## Your privacy, your choice

We use essential cookies to make sure the site can function. We also use
optional cookies for advertising, personalisation of content, usage
analysis, and social media, as well as to allow video information to be
shared for both marketing, analytics and editorial purposes.

By accepting optional cookies, you consent to the processing of your
personal data - including transfers to third parties. Some third parties
are outside of the European Economic Area, with varying standards of
data protection.

See our <a href="https://www.nature.com/info/privacy"
data-cc-action="privacy">privacy policy</a> for more information on the
use of your personal data.

Manage preferences for further information and to change your choices.

Accept all cookies

Reject optional cookies

Thank you for visiting nature.com. You are using a browser version with
limited support for CSS. To obtain the best experience, we recommend you
use a more up to date browser (or turn off compatibility mode in
Internet Explorer). In the meantime, to ensure continued support, we are
displaying the site without styles and JavaScript.

## Abstract

High-harmonic generation (HHG) is a signature optical phenomenon of
strongly driven, nonlinear optical systems. Specifically, the
understanding of the HHG process in rare gases has played a key role in
the development of attosecond
science<sup><a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR1"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e570" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 1"
title="Krausz, F. &amp; Ivanov, M. Attosecond physics. Rev. Mod. Phys. 81, 163–234 (2009).">1</a></sup>.
Recently, HHG has also been reported in solids, providing novel
opportunities such as controlling strong-field and attosecond processes
in dense optical media down to the
nanoscale<sup><a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR2"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e574" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 2"
title="Ghimire, S. &amp; Reis, D. A. High-harmonic generation from solids. Nat. Phys. 15, 10–16 (2019).">2</a></sup>.
Here, we report HHG from a low-loss, indium-doped cadmium oxide thin
film by leveraging the epsilon-near-zero (ENZ)
effect<sup><a href="#ref-CR3" id="ref-link-section-d53767785e578"
data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor"
data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref"
title="Alam, M. Z., De Leon, I. &amp; Boyd, R. W. Large optical nonlinearity of indium tin oxide in its epsilon-near-zero region. Science 352, 795–797 (2016).">3</a>,<a href="#ref-CR4" id="ref-link-section-d53767785e578_1"
data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor"
data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref"
title="Caspani, L. et al. Enhanced nonlinear refractive index in epsilon-near-zero materials. Phys. Rev. Lett. 116, 233901 (2016).">4</a>,<a href="#ref-CR5" id="ref-link-section-d53767785e578_2"
data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor"
data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref"
title="Engheta, N. Pursuing near-zero response. Science 340, 286–287 (2013).">5</a>,<a href="#ref-CR6" id="ref-link-section-d53767785e578_3"
data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor"
data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref"
title="Luk, T. S. et al. Enhanced third harmonic generation from the epsilon-near-zero modes of ultrathin films. Appl. Phys. Lett. 106, 151103 (2015).">6</a>,<a href="#ref-CR7" id="ref-link-section-d53767785e578_4"
data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor"
data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref"
title="Niu, X., Hu, X., Chu, S. &amp; Gong, Q. Epsilon-near-zero photonics: a new platform for integrated devices. Adv. Opt. Mater. 6, 1701292 (2018).">7</a>,<a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR8"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e581" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 8"
title="Yang, Y. et al. Femtosecond optical polarization switching using a cadmium oxide-based perfect absorber. Nat. Photon. 11, 390–395 (2017).">8</a></sup>,
whereby the real part of the material’s permittivity in certain spectral
ranges vanishes, as well as the associated large resonant enhancement of
the driving laser field. We find that ENZ-assisted harmonics exhibit a
pronounced spectral redshift as well as linewidth broadening, resulting
from the photo induced electron heating and the consequent
time-dependent ENZ wavelength of the material. Our results provide a new
platform to study strong-field and ultrafast electron dynamics in ENZ
materials, reveal new degrees of freedom for spectral and temporal
control of HHG, and open up the possibilities of compact solid-state
attosecond light sources.

You have full access to this article via **Princeton University**.

<span class="c-article-meta-recommendations__item-type">Article</span>
<span class="c-article-meta-recommendations__date">16 December
2024</span>

## Main

Although traditionally observed in rare-gas
atoms<sup><a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR9"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e602" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 9"
title="McPherson, A. et al. Studies of multiphoton production of vacuum-ultraviolet radiation in the rare gases. J. Opt. Soc. Am. B 4, 595–601 (1987).">9</a></sup>,
HHG has also recently been reported in a range of solid-state
systems<sup><a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR2"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e606" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 2"
title="Ghimire, S. &amp; Reis, D. A. High-harmonic generation from solids. Nat. Phys. 15, 10–16 (2019).">2</a></sup>
including dielectrics<sup><a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR10"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e610" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 10"
title="Luu, T. T. et al. Extreme ultraviolet high-harmonic spectroscopy of solids. Nature 521, 498–502 (2015).">10</a>,<a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR11"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e613" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 11"
title="Ghimire, S. et al. Observation of high-order harmonic generation in a bulk crystal. Nat. Phys. 7, 138–141 (2010).">11</a></sup>,
semiconductors<sup><a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR12"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e617" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 12"
title="Sivis, M. et al. Tailored semiconductors for high-harmonic optoelectronics. Science 357, 303–306 (2017).">12</a>,<a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR13"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e620" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 13"
title="Liu, H. et al. Enhanced high-harmonic generation from an all-dielectric metasurface. Nat. Phys. 14, 1006–1010 (2018).">13</a></sup>
and emerging two-dimensional
materials<sup><a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR14"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e624" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 14"
title="Liu, H. et al. High-harmonic generation from an atomically thin semiconductor. Nat. Phys. 13, 262–265 (2017).">14</a>,<a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR15"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e627" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 15"
title="Hafez, H. A. et al. Extremely efficient terahertz high-harmonic generation in graphene by hot Dirac fermions. Nature 561, 507–511 (2018).">15</a></sup>,
thus opening up new avenues for solid-state attosecond
spectroscopy<sup><a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR10"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e632" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 10"
title="Luu, T. T. et al. Extreme ultraviolet high-harmonic spectroscopy of solids. Nature 521, 498–502 (2015).">10</a></sup>,
with the additional advantage of producing stable light
waveforms<sup><a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR10"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e636" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 10"
title="Luu, T. T. et al. Extreme ultraviolet high-harmonic spectroscopy of solids. Nature 521, 498–502 (2015).">10</a>,<a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR16"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e639" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 16"
title="Garg, M., Kim, H. Y. &amp; Goulielmakis, E. Ultimate waveform reproducibility of extreme-ultraviolet pulses by high-harmonic generation in quartz. Nat. Photon. 12, 291–296 (2018).">16</a></sup>
in the EUV regime with a compact set-up. Unfortunately, strong
above-bandgap absorption restricts the observed solid-state HHG process
to a very thin layer of the material (typically tens of nanometres in
thickness), significantly limiting the generation
efficiency<sup><a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR17"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e643" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 17"
title="Ghimire, S. et al. Generation and propagation of high-order harmonics in crystals. Phys. Rev. A 85, 043836 (2012).">17</a></sup>.

Nanostructures with plasmonic resonances are widely known to provide
enhancements of the optical near field, and therefore have been
investigated for boosting the HHG efficiency in the surrounding
media<sup><a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR18"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e650" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 18"
title="Han, S. et al. High-harmonic generation by field enhanced femtosecond pulses in metal–sapphire nanostructure. Nat. Commun. 7, 13105 (2016).">18</a>,<a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR19"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e653" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 19"
title="Vampa, G. et al. Plasmon-enhanced high-harmonic generation from silicon. Nat. Phys. 13, 659–662 (2017).">19</a></sup>.
However, plasmonic nanostructures typically exhibit a relatively low
damage threshold under intense laser fields and the electric field
enhancement is restricted to small regions, for example near the apex of
conical structures<sup><a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR18"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e657" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 18"
title="Han, S. et al. High-harmonic generation by field enhanced femtosecond pulses in metal–sapphire nanostructure. Nat. Commun. 7, 13105 (2016).">18</a></sup>.
Furthermore, the periodic arrangement of plasmonic antennas may lead to
multiple diffraction of the generated high-harmonic signal over a wide
range of angles<sup><a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR12"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e661" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 12"
title="Sivis, M. et al. Tailored semiconductors for high-harmonic optoelectronics. Science 357, 303–306 (2017).">12</a>,<a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR19"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e664" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 19"
title="Vampa, G. et al. Plasmon-enhanced high-harmonic generation from silicon. Nat. Phys. 13, 659–662 (2017).">19</a></sup>,
making the collection of high harmonics unwieldy. More recently,
materials that exhibit a vanishing real part of their permittivity in
certain spectral ranges, commonly known as epsilon-near-zero (ENZ)
materials, have been found to exhibit unique nonlinear optical
properties<sup><a href="#ref-CR3" id="ref-link-section-d53767785e668"
data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor"
data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref"
title="Alam, M. Z., De Leon, I. &amp; Boyd, R. W. Large optical nonlinearity of indium tin oxide in its epsilon-near-zero region. Science 352, 795–797 (2016).">3</a>,<a href="#ref-CR4" id="ref-link-section-d53767785e668_1"
data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor"
data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref"
title="Caspani, L. et al. Enhanced nonlinear refractive index in epsilon-near-zero materials. Phys. Rev. Lett. 116, 233901 (2016).">4</a>,<a href="#ref-CR5" id="ref-link-section-d53767785e668_2"
data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor"
data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref"
title="Engheta, N. Pursuing near-zero response. Science 340, 286–287 (2013).">5</a>,<a href="#ref-CR6" id="ref-link-section-d53767785e668_3"
data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor"
data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref"
title="Luk, T. S. et al. Enhanced third harmonic generation from the epsilon-near-zero modes of ultrathin films. Appl. Phys. Lett. 106, 151103 (2015).">6</a>,<a href="#ref-CR7" id="ref-link-section-d53767785e668_4"
data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor"
data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref"
title="Niu, X., Hu, X., Chu, S. &amp; Gong, Q. Epsilon-near-zero photonics: a new platform for integrated devices. Adv. Opt. Mater. 6, 1701292 (2018).">7</a>,<a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR8"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e671" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 8"
title="Yang, Y. et al. Femtosecond optical polarization switching using a cadmium oxide-based perfect absorber. Nat. Photon. 11, 390–395 (2017).">8</a></sup>.
Although not yet explored, ENZ materials are appealing for HHG as the
ENZ effect can greatly boost the pump laser field over the entire volume
of a planar thin film made of the ENZ material. In addition, the deep
sub-wavelength thickness of the film may allow efficient extraction of
the harmonic signal, thus enhancing the HHG efficiency. Moreover, upon
ultrafast high field excitation, the unique hot-electron dynamics of the
ENZ material may lead to intriguing spectral and temporal control of the
high harmonics.

In this Letter, we report harmonic generation up to the ninth order
directly from an ENZ material: In-doped CdO. The sample, as illustrated
in Fig. <a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#Fig1" data-track="click"
data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor">1a</a>, is
formed from a 75-nm-thick In-doped CdO layer epitaxially grown on a
(100)-oriented MgO substrate by high power impulse magnetron
sputtering<sup><a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR20"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e681" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 20"
title="Sachet, E. et al. Dysprosium-doped cadmium oxide as a gateway material for mid-infrared plasmonics. Nat. Mater. 14, 414–420 (2015).">20</a>,<a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR21"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e684" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 21"
title="Kelley, K. P., Sachet, E., Shelton, C. T. &amp; Maria, J. P. High mobility yttrium doped cadmium oxide thin films. APL Mater. 5, 076105 (2017).">21</a></sup>.
The thickness of the CdO film is comparable to its skin depth at the
ultraviolet wavelengths of the high-harmonic radiation (see
<a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#MOESM1" data-track="click"
data-track-label="link"
data-track-action="supplementary material anchor">Supplementary
Information</a>). An additional 200-nm-thick gold film is then coated on
top of the CdO film to increase the confinement of the excitation
optical field in the CdO device layer. The In-doped CdO film has a
measured carrier density of 2.8 × 10<sup>20</sup> cm<sup>−3</sup> and an
electron mobility of 300 cm<sup>2</sup> V<sup>−1</sup> s<sup>−1</sup>.
According to the Drude dispersion formula for this material, the real
part of its permittivity crosses zero at a wavelength of 2.1 μm.

<figure>
<div class="c-article-section__figure-content">
<div class="c-article-section__figure-item">
<img
src="//media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1038%2Fs41567-019-0584-7/MediaObjects/41567_2019_584_Fig1_HTML.png"
aria-describedby="figure-1-desc ai-alt-disclaimer-figure-1-1"
loading="lazy" width="685" height="398"
alt="Fig. 1: Sample schematic and linear optical responses." /><span
id="ai-alt-disclaimer-figure-1-1" class="u-visually-hidden">The
alternative text for this image may have been generated using AI.</span>
<div class="c-article-section__figure-link">
<a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7/figures/1"
class="c-article__pill-button" data-test="article-link"
data-track="click" data-track-label="button"
data-track-action="view figure"
data-track-dest="link:Figure1 Full size image"
aria-label="Full size image figure 1" rel="nofollow"><span>Full size
image</span><img
src="data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB3aWR0aD0iMTYiIGhlaWdodD0iMTYiIGZvY3VzYWJsZT0iZmFsc2UiIHJvbGU9ImltZyIgYXJpYS1oaWRkZW49InRydWUiIGNsYXNzPSJ1LWljb24iPjx1c2UgeG1sbnM6eGxpbms9Imh0dHA6Ly93d3cudzMub3JnLzE5OTkveGxpbmsiIHhsaW5rOmhyZWY9IiNpY29uLWVkcy1pLWNoZXZyb24tcmlnaHQtc21hbGwiPjwvdXNlPjwvc3ZnPg=="
class="u-icon" /></a>
</div>
</div>
<div id="figure-1-desc" class="c-article-section__figure-description"
data-test="bottom-caption">
<p><strong>a</strong>, Schematic of the ENZ sample composed of a MgO
substrate, CdO thin film and gold capping layer. The pump laser is
incident from the substrate side with an incident angle of <em>θ</em> in
free space. <strong>b</strong>, Measured real and imaginary parts of the
permittivity of CdO as a function of wavelength. <strong>c</strong>,
Measured polarization-dependent linear absorption spectra of the sample.
<strong>d</strong>, Field enhancements in CdO for <em>p</em>-polarized
light as a function of wavelength and incident angle calculated from the
transfer matrix method. <em>E</em><sub>0</sub> is the incident electric
field in free space. <strong>e</strong>, Calculated field enhancements
of the three-layer structure as a function of location at wavelength
<em>λ</em> = 2.08 μm and <em>θ</em> = 50° (marked with a red star in
<strong>d</strong>).</p>
</div>
</div>
<figcaption><strong>Fig. 1: Sample schematic and linear optical
responses.</strong></figcaption>
</figure>

The origin of the field enhancement near the material’s ENZ wavelength
can be intuitively understood by the boundary condition of
electromagnetic fields. Specifically, it requires continuity of the
normal displacement field
*ε*<sub>MgO</sub>*E*<sub>MgO</sub> = *ε*<sub>CdO</sub>*E*<sub>CdO</sub>
(*ε*<sub>MgO</sub> and *ε*<sub>CdO</sub> are the permittivities of MgO
and CdO, respectively, and *E*<sub>MgO</sub> and *E*<sub>CdO</sub> are
the normal electric fields at the material boundary). When the real part
of *ε*<sub>CdO</sub> vanishes, *E*<sub>CdO</sub> diverges in the limit
that the imaginary part of *ε*<sub>CdO</sub> approaches zero. In-doped
CdO has an imaginary permittivity of 0.18 at its ENZ wavelength (Fig.
<a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#Fig1" data-track="click"
data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor">1b</a>), which
is about three times lower than the imaginary permittivity of
conventional conductive metal oxides such as ITO at their corresponding
ENZ wavelength<sup><a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR21"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e807" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 21"
title="Kelley, K. P., Sachet, E., Shelton, C. T. &amp; Maria, J. P. High mobility yttrium doped cadmium oxide thin films. APL Mater. 5, 076105 (2017).">21</a>,<a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR22"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e810" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 22"
title="Naik, G. V., Kim, J. &amp; Boltasseva, A. Oxides and nitrides as alternative plasmonic materials in the optical range. Opt. Mater. Express 1, 1090–1099 (2011).">22</a></sup>.
The lower imaginary permittivity of CdO is enabled by its high crystal
quality and higher electron mobility, which is critical for the large
field enhancement. Furthermore, the ENZ wavelength can be varied by
adjusting the doping level of the CdO
film<sup><a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR20"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e814" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 20"
title="Sachet, E. et al. Dysprosium-doped cadmium oxide as a gateway material for mid-infrared plasmonics. Nat. Mater. 14, 414–420 (2015).">20</a></sup>.
Figure <a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#Fig1" data-track="click"
data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor">1c</a>
presents the measured absolute absorption spectra of the sample under
*p*- and *s*-polarized incidence, respectively. The absorption peak
under *p*-polarized incidence can be attributed to the Ferrell–Berreman
resonance, which was first discovered at the ENZ wavelengths of noble
metals<sup><a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR23"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e831" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 23"
title="Stern, E. A. &amp; Ferrell, R. A. Surface plasma oscillations of a degenerate electron gas. Phys. Rev. 120, 130–136 (1960).">23</a></sup>
and polar crystals<sup><a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR24"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e835" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 24"
title="Berreman, D. W. Infrared absorption at longitudinal optic frequency in cubic crystal films. Phys. Rev. 130, 2193–2198 (1963).">24</a></sup>.
Figure <a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#Fig1" data-track="click"
data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor">1d</a>
illustrates the field enhancements as a function of the wavelength and
incidence angle calculated from the transfer matrix method, showing an
intensity enhancement of up to 16-fold when the pump wavelength is tuned
to the ENZ wavelength. The enhancement factor is inversely proportional
to the film thickness<sup><a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR25"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e842" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 25"
title="Campione, S., Brener, I. &amp; Marquier, F. Theory of epsilon-near-zero modes in ultrathin films. Phys. Rev. B 91, 121408 (2015).">25</a></sup>.
Moreover, as illustrated in Fig.
<a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#Fig1" data-track="click"
data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor">1e</a>, the
field enhancement drops off sharply in the regions outside the ENZ
region, indicating that the nonlinear optical response of the structure
is boosted dominantly in the ENZ material itself.

We generate high-harmonic radiation by illuminating the sample at an
angle of incidence *θ* with a linearly polarized, 60 fs laser pulse at a
1 kHz repetition rate. The emitted harmonic radiation propagates along
the specular reflection direction, collinear with the pump beam (see
<a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#Sec2" data-track="click"
data-track-label="link" data-track-action="section anchor">Methods</a>).
Figure <a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#Fig2" data-track="click"
data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor">2a</a> shows
the high-harmonic spectra obtained from the layered structure under *p*-
and *s*-polarized illumination at *θ* = 50°, with the excitation
wavelength *λ*<sub>0</sub> centred at 2.08 μm. For *p*-polarized
illumination, odd-order harmonics are observed from the third to the
ninth order, corresponding to a shortest harmonic wavelength of ~250 nm,
for a vacuum peak intensity of 14.1 GW cm<sup>−2</sup> (corresponding to
a vacuum field strength of 0.33 V nm<sup>−1</sup>). Due to the large
resonant field enhancement in the ENZ material, this excitation
intensity is much weaker than the typical intensity of
~1 TW cm<sup>−2</sup> used for HHG from zinc oxide and
silicon<sup><a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR11"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e886" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 11"
title="Ghimire, S. et al. Observation of high-order harmonic generation in a bulk crystal. Nat. Phys. 7, 138–141 (2010).">11</a>,<a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR13"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e889" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 13"
title="Liu, H. et al. Enhanced high-harmonic generation from an all-dielectric metasurface. Nat. Phys. 14, 1006–1010 (2018).">13</a></sup>.
The greatly reduced pump threshold allows the generation of HHG in the
ENZ material at a much higher repetition rate, potentially leading to a
high-power HHG-based source in the future. In contrast, for
*s*-polarized illumination, a configuration that does not lead to field
enhancements, no harmonic signal above the third order is observed. The
measured third-harmonic yield for *p*-polarized illumination is 482
times greater than that for *s*-polarized illumination. These results
serve as clear evidence that HHG originates in the CdO film and is
enhanced by the ENZ effect. We do not observe even-order harmonics in
any given sample orientation or pump polarization due to the inversion
symmetry of CdO’s cubic crystal
structure<sup><a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR26"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e902" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 26"
title="Cimino, A. &amp; Marezio, M. Lattice parameter and defect structure of cadmium oxide containing foreign atoms. J. Phys. Chem. Solids 17, 57–64 (1960).">26</a></sup>.
In Fig. <a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#Fig2" data-track="click"
data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor">2b</a>, we
show the harmonic yield as a function of the excitation intensity.
Although the third harmonic for *s*-polarized illumination scales as
*I*<sup>*3*</sup>, where *I* is the incident laser intensity (an
indication of a perturbative effect), the third to the seventh harmonics
for *p*-polarized illumination all significantly deviate from
perturbative scaling and exhibit a gradual saturation. The harmonic
yield is reversible after repeated measurements with pump intensity
beyond 11.3 GW cm<sup>−2</sup>, indicating no permanent damage (see
<a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#MOESM1" data-track="click"
data-track-label="link"
data-track-action="supplementary material anchor">Supplementary
Information</a>).

<figure>

<div class="c-article-section__figure-item">

<p><strong>a</strong>, High-harmonic spectrum of the CdO-based
structure. For <em>p</em>-polarized illumination, the spectrum extends
from the third to the ninth order (blue and red line). For
<em>s</em>-polarized illumination, only third-harmonic generation is
observed (orange line). <em><span
class="stix">ℏ</span>ω</em><sub>0</sub> = 0.6 eV is the pump photon
energy. Grey dashed lines, photon energy of <em>N</em> × 0.93 <em><span
class="stix">ℏ</span>ω</em><sub>0</sub>, where <em>N</em> is the
harmonic order, specified by the number in parentheses near the harmonic
peaks. <strong>b</strong>, Harmonic yield as a function of pump
intensity. For <em>p</em>-polarized illumination, the third to the
seventh harmonics all scale non-perturbatively (blue, red and green
dots). For <em>s</em>-polarized illumination, the third-harmonic
generation scales perturbatively (orange dots). Solid lines show the
power scaling law <em>I</em><sup><em>N</em></sup>, and are plotted to
guide the eye.</p>
</div>

<figcaption><strong>Fig. 2: HHG from CdO.</strong></figcaption>
</figure>

To confirm the resonant nature of HHG in the ENZ sample, we measure the
fifth harmonic yield with the central wavelength of the excitation pulse
tuned across the CdO ENZ wavelength of 2.1 µm under an identical vacuum
intensity of 5.6 GW cm<sup>−2</sup> (corresponding to a vacuum field
strength of 0.2 V nm<sup>−1</sup>), as illustrated in Fig.
<a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#Fig3" data-track="click"
data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor">3</a>. Despite
the as-expected resonant enhancement of harmonic yield near the ENZ
wavelength, we note that the peak of the harmonic yield redshifts to an
incident wavelength of 2.13 μm. The redshift of the harmonic peak can be
explained by the unique hot-electron dynamics in CdO, as will be
detailed in the following.

<figure>
<div class="c-article-section__figure-content">
<div class="c-article-section__figure-item">
<img
src="//media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1038%2Fs41567-019-0584-7/MediaObjects/41567_2019_584_Fig3_HTML.png"
aria-describedby="figure-3-desc ai-alt-disclaimer-figure-3-1"
loading="lazy" width="685" height="1070"
alt="Fig. 3: Pump wavelength-dependent HHG." /><span
id="ai-alt-disclaimer-figure-3-1" class="u-visually-hidden">The
alternative text for this image may have been generated using AI.</span>
<div class="c-article-section__figure-link">
<a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7/figures/3"
class="c-article__pill-button" data-test="article-link"

data-track-dest="link:Figure3 Full size image"
aria-label="Full size image figure 3" rel="nofollow"><span>Full size
image</span><img
src="data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB3aWR0aD0iMTYiIGhlaWdodD0iMTYiIGZvY3VzYWJsZT0iZmFsc2UiIHJvbGU9ImltZyIgYXJpYS1oaWRkZW49InRydWUiIGNsYXNzPSJ1LWljb24iPjx1c2UgeG1sbnM6eGxpbms9Imh0dHA6Ly93d3cudzMub3JnLzE5OTkveGxpbmsiIHhsaW5rOmhyZWY9IiNpY29uLWVkcy1pLWNoZXZyb24tcmlnaHQtc21hbGwiPjwvdXNlPjwvc3ZnPg=="
class="u-icon" /></a>
</div>
</div>
<div id="figure-3-desc" class="c-article-section__figure-description"
data-test="bottom-caption">
<p><strong>a</strong>, Fifth-harmonic spectra as a function of
excitation wavelength <em>λ</em><sub>0</sub>, with an identical pump
intensity of 5.6 GW cm<sup>−2</sup> (corresponding to a vacuum field
strength of 0.2 V nm<sup>−1</sup>). <strong>b</strong>, Fifth-harmonic
yield as a function of <em>λ</em><sub>0</sub>. Harmonic yield is
determined by integrating the measured fifth-harmonic spectra.
<em>λ</em><sub>0</sub> is determined by the third-harmonic spectral peak
of a bare silicon wafer multiplied by three. The blue dashed line marks
the perfect absorption wavelength <em>λ</em><sub>PA</sub> of the
structure, and the red dashed line marks the ENZ wavelength
<em>λ</em><sub>ENZ</sub> of CdO.</p>
</div>
</div>
<figcaption><strong>Fig. 3: Pump wavelength-dependent
HHG.</strong></figcaption>
</figure>

On careful examination of the harmonic spectra in Fig.
<a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#Fig2" data-track="click"
data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor">2a</a>, we
find that all the harmonic peaks exhibit substantial, excitation
intensity-dependent, red-shifts away from the expected harmonic photon
energy *N<span class="stix">ℏ</span>ω*<sub>0</sub>, the product of the
fundamental photon energy *<span class="stix">ℏ</span>ω*<sub>0</sub> and
the harmonic order *N*, where *<span class="stix">ℏ</span>* is the
reduced Planck constant. The measured *N*th harmonic peak appears at
approximately *N* × 0.93*<span class="stix">ℏ</span>ω*<sub>0</sub> for
all harmonic orders. In Fig.
<a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#Fig4" data-track="click"
data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor">4a</a>, we
present the measured fifth harmonic spectra from the ENZ sample as a
function of the excitation intensity. The resonantly enhanced harmonic
peak from the ENZ material exhibits a pronounced redshift as the
excitation intensity increases, and the redshift saturates at an
excitation intensity of 11.3 GW cm<sup>−2</sup> (corresponding to a
vacuum field strength of 0.29 V nm<sup>−1</sup>). Moreover, we compare
the third-harmonic spectrum of the layered structure under resonant
*p*-polarized excitation and non-resonant *s*-polarized excitation, and
observe that the resonantly enhanced harmonic peak exhibits a broadening
as large as 1.71 times. This unique feature of linewidth broadening
could be used to tailor or reduce the temporal duration of HHG pulses.

<figure>
<div class="c-article-section__figure-content">
<div class="c-article-section__figure-item">
<img
src="//media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1038%2Fs41567-019-0584-7/MediaObjects/41567_2019_584_Fig4_HTML.png"
aria-describedby="figure-4-desc ai-alt-disclaimer-figure-4-1"
loading="lazy" width="685" height="366"
alt="Fig. 4: HHG spectral shift and broadening." /><span
id="ai-alt-disclaimer-figure-4-1" class="u-visually-hidden">The
alternative text for this image may have been generated using AI.</span>
<div class="c-article-section__figure-link">
<a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7/figures/4"
class="c-article__pill-button" data-test="article-link"
data-track="click" data-track-label="button"
data-track-action="view figure"
data-track-dest="link:Figure4 Full size image"
aria-label="Full size image figure 4" rel="nofollow"><span>Full size
image</span><img
src="data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB3aWR0aD0iMTYiIGhlaWdodD0iMTYiIGZvY3VzYWJsZT0iZmFsc2UiIHJvbGU9ImltZyIgYXJpYS1oaWRkZW49InRydWUiIGNsYXNzPSJ1LWljb24iPjx1c2UgeG1sbnM6eGxpbms9Imh0dHA6Ly93d3cudzMub3JnLzE5OTkveGxpbmsiIHhsaW5rOmhyZWY9IiNpY29uLWVkcy1pLWNoZXZyb24tcmlnaHQtc21hbGwiPjwvdXNlPjwvc3ZnPg=="
class="u-icon" /></a>
</div>
</div>
<div id="figure-4-desc" class="c-article-section__figure-description"
data-test="bottom-caption">
<p><strong>a</strong>, Measured spectra of fifth-harmonic generation
(FHG) for <em>p</em>-polarized illumination as a function of pump
intensity. Black dashed line, photon energy of 5<em><span
class="stix">ℏ</span>ω</em><sub>0</sub>, where <em><span
class="stix">ℏ</span>ω</em><sub>0</sub> = 0.6 eV is the pump photon
energy. <strong>b</strong>, Schematics of the hot-electron dynamics of
CdO on photo excitation. <strong>c</strong>, Calculated spectra of
fifth-harmonic generation for <em>p</em>-polarized illumination as a
function of pump intensity. <strong>d</strong>, The evolution of the
cavity resonance wavelength <em>λ</em><sub>R</sub> as a function of time
<em>t</em>.</p>
</div>
</div>
<figcaption><strong>Fig. 4: HHG spectral shift and
broadening.</strong></figcaption>
</figure>

We attribute the microscopic origin of the spectral redshift and
broadening of the harmonic radiation to the photo induced heating of
electrons in the conduction band and the consequent time-dependent ENZ
wavelength of the CdO film. As schematically illustrated in Fig.
<a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#Fig4" data-track="click"
data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor">4b</a>,
following sub-bandgap optical excitation, conduction band electrons in
CdO are heated to a much higher temperature *T*<sub>e</sub>. The
electron heating leads to a modulation of the effective electron mass in
CdO and a redshift of CdO’s ENZ wavelength, according to the Drude
formula<sup><a href="#ref-CR27" id="ref-link-section-d53767785e1169"
data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor"
data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref"
title="Guo, P., Schaller, R. D., Ketterson, J. B. &amp; Chang, R. P. H. Ultrafast switching of tunable infrared plasmons in indium tin oxide nanorod arrays with large absolute amplitude. Nat. Photon. 10, 267–273 (2016).">27</a>,<a href="#ref-CR28" id="ref-link-section-d53767785e1169_1"
data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor"
data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref"
title="Kane, E. O. Band structure of indium antimonide. J. Phys. Chem. Solids 1, 249–261 (1957).">28</a>,<a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR29"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e1172" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 29"
title="Turchinovich, D., Hvam, J. M. &amp; Hoffmann, M. C. Self-phase modulation of a single-cycle terahertz pulse by nonlinear free-carrier response in a semiconductor. Phys. Rev. B 85, 201304 (2012).">29</a></sup>.
The timescale of the modulation is sub-picosecond, and is comparable to
the dwell time of the excitation pulse inside the CdO
cavity<sup><a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR8"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e1176" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 8"
title="Yang, Y. et al. Femtosecond optical polarization switching using a cadmium oxide-based perfect absorber. Nat. Photon. 11, 390–395 (2017).">8</a></sup>,
thus leading to a unique feature in our system that the excitation pulse
interacts with a time-variant cavity while emitting harmonics. This is
manifested in the observed redshift and linewidth broadening of the
time-averaged HHG intensity profile. Here, we develop a model to
estimate the harmonic spectral peak and bandwidth (see
<a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#MOESM1" data-track="click"
data-track-label="link"
data-track-action="supplementary material anchor">Supplementary
Information</a>). We calculate the time-dependent nonlinear current
following the pump field being enhanced and modulated by the ENZ cavity
at a given excitation intensity and obtain the harmonic spectra from
Fourier transformation of the nonlinear current. Figure
<a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#Fig4" data-track="click"
data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor">4c</a>
presents the calculated fifth-harmonic spectra with increasing
excitation intensities, which show progressively increasing spectral
redshift and broadening, in qualitative agreement with the experimental
results. The time-dependent resonant wavelength of the cavity,
*λ*<sub>R</sub>, is schematically shown in Fig.
<a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#Fig4" data-track="click"
data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor">4d</a>, and is
determined from a pump–probe
measurement<sup><a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR8"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e1194" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 8"
title="Yang, Y. et al. Femtosecond optical polarization switching using a cadmium oxide-based perfect absorber. Nat. Photon. 11, 390–395 (2017).">8</a></sup>.
Such a time-averaged model also predicts a non-Lorentzian line-shape of
the fifth-harmonic spectra, as is observed in the experiment. We observe
a similar spectral redshift and broadening in the third-harmonic spectra
with *p*-polarized incidence (see
<a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#MOESM1" data-track="click"
data-track-label="link"
data-track-action="supplementary material anchor">Supplementary
Information</a>). At moderately high excitation intensities, a second
spectral peak corresponding to non-resonant harmonic generation emerges
at a photon energy close to 5<span class="stix">ℏ</span>*ω*<sub>0</sub>,
which is attributed to harmonic emission before the buildup of the
resonant cavity.

The temporal and spectral properties of high harmonics can be further
manipulated, for example by introducing a frequency chirp to the pump
pulse and matching the instantaneous laser frequency to the temporal
evolution of the ENZ frequency. HHG can also serve as an ultrafast probe
into unique nonlinear processes in various ENZ materials. The ENZ
frequency of CdO is tunable from the near- to mid-infrared by adjusting
its doping level either chemically or by electrostatic gating, which
means that experiments can be performed at other wavelengths. Finally,
because conductive metal oxides such as CdO can be integrated within
silicon photonics<sup><a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#ref-CR30"
id="ref-link-section-d53767785e1212" data-track="click"
data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link"
data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 30"
title="Wood, M. G. et al. Gigahertz speed operation of epsilon-near-zero silicon photonic modulators. Optica 5, 233–236 (2018).">30</a></sup>,
there is a possibility of ultra-compact on-chip EUV and attosecond light
sources.

## Methods

### HHG measurements

The experimental set-up for measuring the HHG from the ENZ material is
shown schematically in the Supplementary
<a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#MOESM1" data-track="click"
data-track-label="link"
data-track-action="supplementary material anchor">Information</a>. The
light source is an optical parametric amplifier (OPA, HE-TOPAS-Prime,
Light Conversion) pumped by a Ti:sapphire amplifier system (Legend Elite
Duo, Coherent) operating at a 1 kHz repetition rate. The 60 fs idler
pulse from the OPA, which is tunable from 2.0 to 2.2 µm in wavelength,
is loosely focused into the sample at a ~50° angle of incidence. The
area of the focal spot on the CdO surface was estimated to be
~0.0297 cm<sup>2</sup> from a knife edge measurement. The high-harmonic
beam propagating along the specular reflection direction is collected by
a lens and focused into the entrance slit of a spectrometer, which
consists of a grating-based monochromator (Acton, VM-504, grating:
300 grooves mm<sup>−1</sup>) and a charge-coupled device (CCD, Andor
DO440). Spectrally resolved high-harmonic signals emerging from the ENZ
material are recorded by the CCD. The polarization of the idler pulse is
tuned by a half-waveplate and the excitation intensity is controlled by
a variable neutral density (ND) filter. The reported spectral signals
are not corrected for the efficiency of the spectrometer at different
wavelengths or for *s*- or *p*-polarized pump pulses. To characterize
the pump-to-harmonic energy conversion efficiency, we spatially isolated
the third-harmonic beam using a prism and measured the energy by a
photodiode power sensor (Thorlabs S120VC) at a range of pump energies at
2.08 μm. Higher-order harmonic energies were estimated from the measured
third-harmonic energy by referring to Fig.
<a href="/articles/s41567-019-0584-7#Fig2" data-track="click"
data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor">2b</a>. The
lower bound of the energy conversion efficiency for the third, fifth and
seventh harmonics were determined to be on the order of 10<sup>−5</sup>,
10<sup>−8</sup> and 10<sup>−10</sup>, respectively, at a pump intensity
of 11.3 GW cm<sup>−2</sup>. The loss of the generated harmonic signal in
the optical path was not taken into account.

## Data availability

The data that support the plots within this paper and other findings of
this study are available from the corresponding authors on reasonable
request.

### Contributions

Y.Y. conceived the idea. J.L., Y.Y., T.S.L. and H.L. carried out optical
measurements. A.M. developed the theory. K.K., J.-P.M. and E.L.R.
prepared the sample. All authors contributed to analysing the data and
writing the manuscript. I.B., S.G., M.B.S. and J.-P.M. supervised the
project.
